@@4797617 <p> Now the inconceivable is moving into the realm of the possible -- and increasingly , the probable . <p> If carbon emissions continue to rise unabated , the extinction of the polar bear looms today as a shocking but very real possibility . The largest bear species in the world ( Ursus maritimus ; literally , " sea bear " ) is now in danger of being permanently deleted from the planet 's mega fauna roster . <p> According to a study released last week in the online scientific journal PLOS One , the wide swath of Canada 's Arctic islands could possibly be ice-less for up to five months annually by century 's end owing to climate change , spurring starvation and reproductive collapse for the Arctic 's top predator . <p> The breaking up of sea ice is literally the dismemberment of the foundation of Arctic marine life , the ecosystem on which bears depend . As noted by the World Wildlife Fund ( WWF ) , which co-sponsored the study , the ice is much more than a mere platform for migration and hunting @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ their cubs . Sea ice is also an essential resting and breeding area for their primary food , ringed seals . <p> The study , spearheaded by researchers at the University of Alberta , notes that while the Canadian Arctic Archipelago , home to 20 per cent of the world 's polar bears , has sea ice throughout the year , that situation is rapidly changing . ( Canada on the whole is home to approximately two-thirds of all polar bears worldwide . ) By 2070 , the research suggests , more than 80 per cent of the archipelago 's ice could break up by July each year , prompting pregnant females to retreat to land prematurely , lacking sufficient fat reserves to support pregnancy and nursing . <p> In short , they and their future offspring will simply , tragically , starve into extinction . <p> As University of Alberta scientist Andrew Derocher , co-author of the study , tersely commented , " it does n't look too good " for the future of the polar bears in light of rising global temperatures , which may see a change of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Canada is signatory to the 1973 International Agreement on the Conservation of Polar Bears and the Inuvialuit-Inupiat Polar Bear Management Agreement , and claims to be a world leader in polar bear conservation , its callous approach to climate change potentially undermines its sanguine conservation efforts . <p> According to David Miller , president and CEO of WWF-Canada , the erasure of the polar bear " does n't have to happen . " In a recent interview , the former Toronto mayor hoped that the " iconic " status of the polar bear might prompt many to positive action . For Miller , the polar bear is a " beautiful " creature , symbolizing core Canadian values of wild beauty and graceful perseverance despite adversity and a harsh climate . <p> Miller hopes that Canadians can " take a lesson " from the collapse of the cod fishery and move to preserve a precious species that has meant so much to the lifeblood , imagination and livelihood of the Canadian people . <p> Noting that the City of Toronto decreased its greenhouse gas emissions by 15 per cent less than1990 levels @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to clean energy , if we " find the gumption " to develop of a national strategy for such a move . <p> Such fealty to the polar bear apparently even has an acceptable price tag . <p> As reported in these pages , according to a recent report commissioned by Environment Canada , Canadians would be willing to annually fork over $508 per household ( $6.3 billion per year ) to keep the polar bears prowling the Arctic . <p> From soft drink ads to Canada 's toonie , the polar bear has been emblazoned in our consciousness and become an emblem of Canada 's North . <p> Just as the prospect of losing the bald eagle , the avian symbol of the United States , helped goad the U.S. government to ban DDT use decades ago following upon Rachel Carson 's Silent Spring , the possibility of losing the iconic polar bear , symbol of a wild and enduring Canadian North , might , let us hope , prompt Canada to shift from delinquency to leadership on global climate change . <p> Not only polar bears but our national @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 
@@4797619 <p> " Unfortunately , most ( carbon capture ) projects in the pipeline are not getting out of the pipeline . They 've been dying recently . " <p> Howard Herzog <p> senior research engineer at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology <p> LONGYEARBYEN , NORWAY -- Mine 7 is a broad black smear across the pure white Arctic wilderness that surrounds the world 's most northern town . <p> For more than a century , tonnes of coal carved out of the mountain face have fuelled the furnaces that generate electricity for this community of 2,000 people . <p> The power plant 's towering smokestack spews out a steady , grey cloud that falls as soot . Mixed with coal dust from the mine , it turns snow black . <p> It is a stain not just on the landscape , but on the reputation Norway prefers to project to the world of a green nation leading international efforts against climate change . <p> Coal has been part of life in Longyearbyen since the turn of the 20th century . American industrialist John Longyear came here in 1906 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ once covered what is now the Svalbard archipelago . <p> Coal is still the easiest source of power and heat this far north . <p> Experts at University Centre in Svalbard , the world 's most northern university , think they have the perfect alternative : capture carbon dioxide from the coal plant 's smokestack , bury it deep beneath the permafrost , and make Longyearbyen a carbon neutral showcase . <p> " This is the only coal-fired power plant in Norway , so it 's something we 're not really proud about , " says Sebastian Sikora , managing director of the UNIS project . " The idea is just to cut the carbon-dioxide emissions from this chimney . " <p> As a concept , carbon capture and storage is backed by the International Panel on Climate Change , the body of leading international scientists assessing the impact of global warming . <p> Researchers say the technology was proved long ago . But building demonstration projects costs billions of dollars and requires government subsidies in numerous countries , including Canada . <p> Large-scale carbon capture and storage ca n't make @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , says Howard Herzog , a senior research engineer at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology . <p> " You need to create a market , " he adds . <p> Governments can do that in a variety of ways , including a carbon tax or a cap-and-trade system that sets limits on emissions and provides economic incentives for companies . <p> The World Bank is leading a United Nations push to put a price on carbon , and has won the support of 74 countries , representing 54 per cent of global greenhouse gas emissions and more than half the world 's gross domestic product . <p> More than 1,000 companies and investors have also signed on . So have British Columbia , Alberta and Quebec . But the Harper government refuses to back the World Bank 's carbon pricing campaign . <p> At least 18 projects to capture and store carbon have been announced in 13 countries , including Canada , China , Norway and the U.S. , according to the MIT program tracking the technology . <p> " Unfortunately , most projects in the pipeline are not getting out @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 've been dying recently . " <p> Saskatchewan started the world 's first commercial-scale carbon and capture storage facility on Oct. 2 as part of a new coal-fired power plant . The cost is expected to exceed $1.4 billion . <p> SaskPower , which is owned by the province , received a $240-million federal subsidy for the Boundary Dam project . The goal is to capture one million tonnes of carbon a year , which the company says is the equivalent of taking 250,000 cars off the roads . <p> The world 's second major carbon capture operation is going up in Kemper County , Miss . The cost has ballooned from a planned $2.7 billion to $6.3 billion . The plant 's opening has been postponed to next May . <p> Both the Canadian and American plants expect to make money selling some of the captured carbon dioxide to oil companies . They want to inject the gas into oil wells to increase the amount of crude forced to the surface , locking most of the carbon dioxide deep underground . The process is called enhanced oil recovery . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's commitments to cut greenhouse gas emissions , has spent more than $580 million on carbon capture and storage research and development . <p> As well , the Alberta government and Ottawa have poured $865 million into an estimated $1.35-billion Shell project to capture carbon dioxide emissions from upgrading oilsands bitumen into synthetic crude . <p> Set to start operating next year , the Quest project is designed to capture up to 35 per cent of carbon dioxide produced at the Scotford Upgrader , outside Fort Saskatchewan , northeast of Edmonton . <p> Once the greenhouse gas is compressed into a dense fluid , it will be pumped through a pipeline running 60 kilometres north of Fort Saskatchewan . There it will be injected into three special wells , down into porous rock more than two kilometres underground . <p> Yet the expensive technology 's future is n't bright in Alberta under new Premier Jim Prentice , who was Harper 's former environment minister . Prentice announced in early October that he wo n't spend more tax dollars supporting carbon capture and storage , calling it " a science experiment . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ coal is burned each year to produce electricity and heat . <p> The current plant was built in 1983 and frequently breaks down under the increasing load as Longyearbyen becomes a bigger draw for tourists , scientists and university students . Six diesel generators kick in when the town needs backup power . <p> The project to capture and store carbon dioxide from the plant started in 2007 . It 's stalled at the fourth and final stage , while politicians debate what to do with the coal-fired facility , which is too old and dirty for modern equipment . Climate change scientists warn that there 's little time left for sharp cuts in greenhouse gas emissions if they 're to have a significant impact on global warming . <p> In Longyearbyen , there is added urgency . <p> Two years ago , an early-morning fire erupted in the coal-fired plant . The blaze was quickly extinguished , so the plant shutdown was short . Authorities said there was enough diesel stockpiled to keep the town running on generator power for 18 days . <p> If the Arctic wind had picked @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ out the control room , or if colder weather had struck , the emergency would have been much more severe . @@4797674 <p> The Polar Sea -- a 10-part TV series beginning Monday on TVO -- is a thrilling epic that takes us on a magical 10,000-kilometre mystery tour through the legendary Northwest Passage . Hugely entertaining and mind-bogglingly educational at the same time , it instantly stands as one of the landmarks in the long and rich history of Canadian documentary filmmaking . <p> The Arctic turf the series explores is not merely historic . It 's about one of the key environmental issues of today : climate change . The Polar Sea , using up-to-the-minute camera technology , reveals a world that is being transformed by melting . The Arctic is the place on Earth where the pace of global warming is most extreme and where the impact is most dramatic . <p> Viewers get a close-up view of just how human communities and many other forms of life are being forever changed and threatened by the disappearance of Arctic ice . And we get not only the scientific evidence , but memorable vignettes about those whose lives will never be the same . <p> And we get @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The great and resonant Pinsent voice has never been used more effectively , although we never see his face . <p> Almost as amazing as the history-and-adventure saga unfolding on the screen is the risky , behind-the-scenes tale about the making of this series , in circumstances that were way beyond chancey . One key story thread is the quest of a few guys who set out to make the long trek from Iceland to Alaska through the notoriously dangerous passage on a tiny boat . <h> Related : <h> Television <p> But how can you manage a 10-part series when there 's no telling what might happen to these amateurs and their boat along the way ? <p> " We did n't know the story was going to work out , " Kevin McMahon , the writer , creative director and co-producer of the series , confessed one morning at a Bloor St. caf ? near his office at Primitive Entertainment . " It nearly killed me . This could have been a huge failure . That 's what makes documentary so exhilarating when it succeeds . But the risk is what makes us grateful for the broadcasters who had the guts to take it . " <p> It 's a coup for TVO , which invested a chunk of the project 's $2.5 million budget , to have the world premiere of The Polar Sea , shown without commercials on weeknights for the next two weeks starting Monday at 9 p.m . The anchor was Arte , a network operating in both Germany and France , specializing in documentaries and children 's programs . <p> " Wolfgang Bergmann conceived it and was looking for filmmakers who could pull it off , " Kevin explains . <p> In the fall of 2011 , they met . Bergmann wanted to use the Northwest Passage as the theme because that 's what Europeans know about . " At first I thought , ' I do n't @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ opening episode begins in Reykjavik , Iceland , where we meet Richard Tegner , a Swedish amateur about to embark on an expedition . From the 15th century onward , the search for the Northwest Passage was the biggest and most dangerous challenge for European navies . Now , the great glacier melt has made the passage a popular route for cruise ships and an adventure for amateurs . <p> The revelations that pop up along the way are what make it impossible to stop watching this series once you 've started . <p> Episode 5 , called " Arctic Exiles " ( Friday at 9 p.m. on TVO ) , is one of my favourites . We learn the story of past disasters , when whole communities faced disaster by moving from places where they knew how to live to places where survival was much more of a challenge . The current situation offers a mirror to historic tragedy , even as the drama is enhanced by music from Inuit throat singer Celina Kalluk . He mocked his friend and contemporary Robert Flaherty , who made the silent classic Nanook of the North ( 1922 ) documenting life in the Arctic . During a debate about whose approach was better , Grierson once told Flaherty : " OK , Bob , you go and make a film about the savage at the end of the Earth . I 'll do the savage in Birmingham . " <p> Rather than depict life near the North Pole , Grierson wanted to show his audience what was close to home . But today , the North seems much closer to home and I feel sure that Grierson would have felt proud and astonished by the Canadians who got The Polar Sea onto the screen . <p> Inevitably , some episodes are stronger than others . But as a total experience , The Polar Sea is overwhelming and essential viewing .
